=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
qwen

=== SOURCE CODE (cpp) ===

#include <bitset>
#include <sstream>
#include <iomanip>

#include <string>

class NumberConverter {
public:
    static std::string decimal_to_binary(int decimal_num);
    static int binary_to_decimal(const std::string& binary_num);
    static std::string decimal_to_octal(int decimal_num);
    static int octal_to_decimal(const std::string& octal_num);
    static std::string decimal_to_hex(int decimal_num);
    static int hex_to_decimal(const std::string& hex_num);
};

std::string NumberConverter::decimal_to_binary(int decimal_num) {
    std::string binary_num = std::bitset<32>(decimal_num).to_string();
    binary_num.erase(0, binary_num.find_first_not_of('0')); 
    return binary_num.empty() ? "0" : binary_num;
}

int NumberConverter::binary_to_decimal(const std::string& binary_num) {
    return std::stoi(binary_num, nullptr, 2);
}

std::string NumberConverter::decimal_to_octal(int decimal_num) {
    std::stringstream ss;
    ss << std::oct << decimal_num;
    return ss.str();
}

int NumberConverter::octal_to_decimal(const std::string& octal_num) {
    return std::stoi(octal_num, nullptr, 8);
}

std::string NumberConverter::decimal_to_hex(int decimal_num) {
    std::stringstream ss;
    ss << std::hex << decimal_num;
    return ss.str();
}

int NumberConverter::hex_to_decimal(const std::string& hex_num) {
    return std::stoi(hex_num, nullptr, 16);
}

- Keep behavior identical, including inputs/outputs, side effects, edge cases, and exceptions.
- Use idiomatic py only when it does not change behavior.

```cpp

#include <bitset>
#include <sstream>
#include <iomanip>

#include <string>

class NumberConverter {
public:
    static std::string decimal_to_binary(int decimal_num);
    static int binary_to_decimal(const std::string& binary_num);
    static std::string decimal_to_octal(int decimal_num);
    static int octal_to_decimal(const std::string& octal_num);
    static std::string decimal_to_hex(int decimal_num);
    static int hex_to_decimal(const std::string& hex_num);
};

std::string NumberConverter::decimal_to_binary(int decimal_num) {
    std::string binary_num = std::bitset<32>(decimal_num).to_string();
    binary_num.erase(0, binary_num.find_first_not_of('0')); 
    return binary_num.empty() ? "0" : binary_num;
}

int NumberConverter::binary_to_decimal(const std::string& binary_num) {
    return std::stoi(binary_num, nullptr, 2);
}

std::string NumberConverter::decimal_to_octal(int decimal_num) {
    std::stringstream ss;
    ss << std::oct << decimal_num;
    return ss.str();
}

int NumberConverter::octal_to_decimal(const std::string& octal_num) {
    return std::stoi(octal_num, nullptr, 8);
}

std::string NumberConverter::decimal_to_hex(int decimal_num) {
    std::stringstream ss;
    ss << std::hex << decimal_num;
    return ss.str();
}

int NumberConverter::hex_to_decimal(const std::string& hex_num) {
    return std::stoi(hex_num, nullptr, 16);
}

```

The subject sequence is the longest initial subsequence of the input string, starting with the first non-white-space character, that is of the expected form. The expected form is:
- optional sign
- then either:
  - a sequence of digits in the given base
  - or, if base is 0 or 16, a 0x/0X prefix and a sequence of hexadecimal digits
  - or, if base is 0 or 8, a 0 prefix and a sequence of octal digits
But since base is fixed (2,8,16), we can simplify.

For base 16: The expected form is optional sign, then either a sequence of hex digits, or a 0x/0X prefix followed by a sequence of hex digits. The longest such prefix. Since "0x" prefix includes a '0' which is a hex digit, the longest valid prefix for "0x" alone is "0" (because "0x" prefix + empty digits is not valid). So we can implement by scanning: after sign, if base 16 and next two chars are "0x"/"0X", we tentatively consume them, then scan hex digits. If we find at least one hex digit, then the prefix is part of the number. If we find zero hex digits, then we should not consume the prefix; instead, we should parse starting from the '0' as a hex digit. This is equivalent to: if the string starts with "0x" and the character after 'x' is a hex digit, consume "0x" and then digits; otherwise, just parse digits normally. But what about "0xg"? After "0x", 'g' is not hex digit, so no digits after prefix. Then the longest valid prefix is "0" (the '0' before x). So parse "0", stop at 'x'. So algorithm: after sign, if base==16 and s starts with '0x'/'0X' and the next char after that is a valid hex digit, then consume the prefix and then consume all hex digits. Else, consume all hex digits from current position. This handles "0x" and "0xg" as "0". For "0x10", consumes prefix and digits -> 16. For "0x1g", consumes "0x1" -> 1, stops at g. Good.

For base 8: The expected form is optional sign, then either a sequence of octal digits, or a 0 prefix and a sequence of octal digits. But a leading 0 is just an octal digit, so no special prefix needed. For base 8, "0o" is not a prefix. So just scan octal digits.

For base 2: just scan binary digits.

For base 10: not needed but could.

Now, after scanning digits, if no digits were consumed (after sign and optional prefix), invalid_argument. If digits were consumed, compute value. Also, std::stoi skips leading whitespace. Python int also skips whitespace, but only ASCII? Actually Python int accepts unicode whitespace? It uses `str.strip()` which removes whitespace. But we'll implement our own to match C++.

Let's write a helper:

```python
def _stoi(s, base):
    i = 0
    n = len(s)
    # skip leading whitespace (C locale)
    while i < n and s[i] in ' \t\n\v\f\r':
        i += 1
    if i >= n:
        raise ValueError("invalid_argument")
    sign = 1
    if s[i] == '+':
        i += 1
    elif s[i] == '-':
        sign = -1
        i += 1
    start = i
    # For base 16, handle 0x prefix if followed by hex digit
    if base == 16 and i + 1 < n and s[i] == '0' and s[i+1] in 'xX':
        # check if char after prefix is hex digit
        if i + 2 < n and _is_hex_digit(s[i+2]):
            i += 2
        # else leave i at '0'
    # collect valid digits
    digits_start = i
    while i < n and _is_digit(s[i], base):
        i += 1
    if i == digits_start:
        raise ValueError("invalid_argument")
    digit_str = s[digits_start:i]
    # compute value
    value = 0
    for ch in digit_str:
        value = value * base + _digit_value(ch)
    value *= sign
    # check int range
    if value < -2147483648 or value > 2147483647:
        raise OverflowError("out_of_range")
    return value
```

But wait: For base 16, if string is "0x" and no hex digit after prefix, we leave i at '0', then digits_start = i, and we collect '0' as digit. Good. If string is "0x10", we consume prefix, digits_start after prefix, collect "10". Good. If string is "0X1g", consume "0X", collect "1", stop at g. Good.

But what about string "0x" with sign? "-0x": sign consumed, i at '0', prefix check sees '0' and 'x', but no digit after x, so leave i at '0', collect '0' -> value 0. Is that what C++ does? Let's verify: strtol("-0x", &end, 16) likely returns 0 with endptr pointing to 'x'? I think yes. So good.

What about string "0x+1"? After prefix, '+' is not hex digit, so prefix not consumed, collect '0', stop at 'x'. value 0. C++? strtol("0x+1", base 16): '0' is digit, 'x' invalid, returns 0. Yes.

What about string "  -0x10"? skip whitespace, sign -, prefix consumed, digits "10" -> 16, value -16. Good.

Now, for base 2 and 8, we don't handle prefix. For base 8, "0o10": collect '0' (valid octal), stop at 'o', value 0. C++ strtol("0o10", base 8) returns 0 and endptr at 'o'. Yes.

For base 2, "0b10": collect '0', stop at 'b', value 0. C++ returns 0. Good.

Now, what about strings with leading zeros? Fine.

What about whitespace after digits? We stop at whitespace, return value. C++ does same. Python int would reject trailing whitespace? So Python int allows surrounding whitespace. But our helper also stops at whitespace, so it returns value. Good.

What about strings with plus sign: "+101" -> 5. Good.

What about strings with only sign: "+" -> invalid_argument. Good.

Now, decimal_to_binary: Need to handle negative numbers. C++ bitset<32> gives 32-bit two's complement. For decimal_num = -2147483648 (INT_MIN), bitset<32> gives 0x80000000 -> "10000000000000000000000000000000". Python format(-2147483648 & 0xFFFFFFFF, '032b') -> "10000000000000000000000000000000". Good. For decimal_num = 2147483647 -> "01111111111111111111111111111111" -> lstrip -> "1111111111111111111111111111111" (31 ones). Good.

But wait: C++ bitset<32> for a negative int uses the two's complement representation of the int value. In C++, converting negative int to unsigned long long for bitset? Actually bitset<32>(decimal_num) where decimal_num is int: The constructor takes unsigned long long. The int is converted to unsigned long long by integral conversion: if negative, it's converted by repeatedly adding 2^64 until in range, then bitset takes the low 32 bits. So yes, low 32 bits of two's complement. Python `decimal_num & 0xFFFFFFFF` gives the same for any integer? For negative, Python bitwise AND with 0xFFFFFFFF gives the low 32 bits of the infinite two's complement representation. Yes.

So decimal_to_binary:
```python
@staticmethod
def decimal_to_binary(decimal_num):
    binary_num = format(decimal_num & 0xFFFFFFFF, '032b').lstrip('0')
    return binary_num if binary_num else "0"
```
But wait: For decimal_num = 0, lstrip('0') -> "" -> "0". Good. For decimal_num = -1, format(-1 & 0xFFFFFFFF, '032b') -> "11111111111111111111111111111111", lstrip -> same. Good.

decimal_to_hex: `format(decimal_num, 'x')`. For negative, format(-10, 'x') -> '-a'. C++ std::hex << -10 -> "-a". Good.

Now, need to implement `_is_digit` and `_digit_value`. For base 2: '0','1'. For base 8: '0'-'7'. For base 16: '0'-'9', 'a'-'f', 'A'-'F'. We can use `int(ch, base)`? But for base 16, int('a', 16) works. But we need to check validity. We can use a helper:

```python
def _is_digit(ch, base):
    if base == 2:
        return ch in '01'
    elif base == 8:
        return '0' <= ch <= '7'
    elif base == 16:
        return ch in '0123456789abcdefABCDEF'
    else:
        return ch.isdigit() and int(ch) < base
```

But for base 16, we need to handle uppercase. We can use `int(ch, base)` but it may raise ValueError. Better to use a string of valid digits.

```python
def _is_digit(ch, base):
    if base == 2:
        return ch == '0' or ch == '1'
    if base == 8:
        return '0' <= ch <= '7'
    if base == 16:
        return ch in '0123456789abcdefABCDEF'
    return False
```

`_digit_value`:
```python
def _digit_value(ch):
    if '0' <= ch <= '9':
        return ord(ch) - ord('0')
    if 'a' <= ch <= 'f':
        return ord(ch) - ord('a') + 10
    if 'A' <= ch <= 'F':
        return ord(ch) - ord('A') + 10
    raise ValueError
```

Now, let's think about `decimal_to_binary` for negative numbers. C++ bitset<32>(-1) gives 32 ones. Our format(-1 & 0xFFFFFFFF, '032b') gives 32 ones. Good. That may be fine. But if we want to mimic C++ exactly, we should perhaps restrict to 32-bit signed int? The C++ function takes an int, so if called with a value outside int range, it's undefined? Now, class methods: In Python, we can use `@staticmethod` or `@classmethod`. The C++ static methods are called as `NumberConverter::decimal_to_binary(...)`. In Python, we can call `NumberConverter.decimal_to_binary(...)`. We'll implement as static methods.

Now, need to output only the translated py code in a code block. The code should include the helper functions and class.

Let's write the code:

```python
class NumberConverter:
    @staticmethod
    def decimal_to_binary(decimal_num):
        binary_num = format(decimal_num & 0xFFFFFFFF, '032b').lstrip('0')
        return binary_num if binary_num else "0"

    @staticmethod
    def binary_to_decimal(binary_num):
        return _stoi(binary_num, 2)

    @staticmethod
    def decimal_to_octal(decimal_num):
        return format(decimal_num, 'o')

    @staticmethod
    def octal_to_decimal(octal_num):
        return _stoi(octal_num, 8)

    @staticmethod
    def decimal_to_hex(decimal_num):
        return format(decimal_num, 'x')

    @staticmethod
    def hex_to_decimal(hex_num):
        return _stoi(hex_num, 16)
```

Now define `_stoi` before class or after? In Python, functions can be defined after class if called at runtime. But for clarity, define before class.

Let's write `_stoi` carefully.

```python
def _stoi(s, base):
    i = 0
    n = len(s)
    # skip leading whitespace (C locale)
    while i < n and s[i] in ' \t\n\v\f\r':
        i += 1
    if i >= n:
        raise ValueError("invalid_argument")
    sign = 1
    if s[i] == '+':
        i += 1
    elif s[i] == '-':
        sign = -1
        i += 1
    # For base 16, handle optional 0x/0X prefix if followed by a hex digit
    if base == 16 and i + 1 < n and s[i] == '0' and s[i+1] in 'xX':
        if i + 2 < n and _is_digit(s[i+2], 16):
            i += 2
    digits_start = i
    while i < n and _is_digit(s[i], base):
        i += 1
    if i == digits_start:
        raise ValueError("invalid_argument")
    digit_str = s[digits_start:i]
    value = 0
    for ch in digit_str:
        value = value * base + _digit_value(ch)
    value *= sign
    if value < -2147483648 or value > 2147483647:
        raise OverflowError("out_of_range")
    return value
```

Wait, there's a subtle bug: In the prefix check, we check `if i + 2 < n and _is_digit(s[i+2], 16): i += 2`. But what if the string is "0x" and there is no char after x, but i+2 == n, condition false, so we don't consume prefix. Then digits_start = i (at '0'), collect '0'. Good. What if the string is "0x0" and i+2 < n, s[i+2]='0' hex digit, consume prefix. Then digits_start = i (now at '0' after x), collect '0'. Good. What if the string is "0x1" and i+2 < n, s[i+2]='1', consume prefix. Good.

But what if the string is "0x" and there is a hex digit after x but the digit is at position i+2? That's exactly the condition. Good.

Now, what about string "0x" with base 16 and there is a hex digit after x but the string is "0x" followed by a null? Not possible.

Now, what about string "0x" with base 16 and there is a hex digit after x but the string is "0x1" and i+2 < n true. Good.

Now, what about string "0x" with base 16 and there is a hex digit after x but the string is "0x" and i+2 == n, so no digit. Good.

Now, what about string "0x" with base 16 and there is a hex digit after x but the string is "0x " (space after x): i+2 < n, s[i+2]=' ' not hex digit, so don't consume prefix, collect '0'. Good.

Now, what about string "0x" with base 16 and there is a hex digit after x but the string is "0x\t1": i+2 < n, s[i+2]='\t' not hex digit, don't consume prefix, collect '0'. Good.

Now, what about string "0x" with base 16 and there is a hex digit after x but the string is "0x+1": i+2 < n, s[i+2]='+' not hex digit, don't consume prefix, collect '0'. Good.

Now, what about string "0x" with base 16 and there is a hex digit after x but the string is "0x-1": same.

Now, what about string "0x" with base 16 and there is a hex digit after x but the string is "0x0x": i+2 < n, s[i+2]='0' hex digit, consume prefix, digits_start at '0' (the one after x), collect '0', stop at 'x'. Good.

Now, what about string "0x" with base 16 and there is a hex digit after x but the string is "0x0" and i+2 < n true. Good.

Now, what about string "0x" with base 16 and there is a hex digit after x but the string is "0x" and i+2 == n, so no digit. Good.

Now, what about string "0x" with base 16 and there is a hex digit after x but the string is "0x" and i+2 < n but s[i+2] is a valid hex digit? That's the normal case.

Now, one more nuance: What if the string is "0x" and there is a hex digit after x but the string is "0x" and i+2 < n but s[i+2] is a valid hex digit, but the prefix is actually "0X" uppercase? We check `s[i+1] in 'xX'`. Good.

Now, what about string "0X" with base 16 and no digit after X: condition false, collect '0'. Good.

Now, what about string "0X1": condition true, consume prefix, collect '1'. Good.

Now, what about string "0Xg": condition false, collect '0'. Good.

Now, all good.

Now, let's test some examples mentally.

- `NumberConverter.decimal_to_binary(10)` -> format(10 & 0xFFFFFFFF, '032b') = "00000000000000000000000000001010". lstrip('0') -> "1010". return "1010". C++ returns "1010". Good.

- `NumberConverter.decimal_to_binary(0)` -> "000...000". lstrip -> "" -> "0". Good.

- `NumberConverter.decimal_to_binary(-1)` -> format(-1 & 0xFFFFFFFF, '032b') = "11111111111111111111111111111111". lstrip -> same. Good.

- `NumberConverter.binary_to_decimal("1010")` -> _stoi("1010", 2): skip whitespace, sign none, digits_start 0, collect "1010", value 10, return 10. Good.

- `NumberConverter.binary_to_decimal("0b1010")` -> _stoi: i=0, sign none, base 2 no prefix, digits_start 0, collect '0', stop at 'b', value 0. C++ stoi("0b1010", nullptr, 2) returns 0. Good.

- `NumberConverter.binary_to_decimal("  -1010")` -> skip whitespace, sign -, digits "1010", value -10. Good.

- `NumberConverter.binary_to_decimal("1010  ")` -> collect "1010", stop at space, value 10. Good.

- `NumberConverter.hex_to_decimal("0X1")` -> 1. Good.

- `NumberConverter.hex_to_decimal("  -0x10")` -> skip whitespace, sign -, prefix check true, collect "10", value -16. Good.

Now, one potential issue: In `_stoi`, after consuming the 0x prefix, we set `digits_start = i`. But what if the string is "0x" and we didn't consume prefix, digits_start = i (at '0'), collect '0'. Now, let's think about the "short stable mapping path" and "trap scan". The main trap is `std::stoi` partial parsing vs Python int strict parsing. We handled by implementing `_stoi`. Another trap is bitset<32> for negative numbers. We handled with `& 0xFFFFFFFF`. Another trap is octal/hex negative sign. Python format handles.

Now, what about `std::stoi` for base 16 with string "0x" and no digits after prefix: We chose to parse '0'. Let's double-check with actual C++ standard. I want to be certain. Let's recall from cppreference for `std::stoi`: "The string is converted to an integer value in the given base. The conversion is performed as if by `std::strtol`." `strtol` behavior: "The subject sequence is the longest initial subsequence of the input string, starting with the first non-white-space character, that is of the expected form. The expected form is: an optional plus or minus sign, then one of: a sequence of digits in the given base, or a 0x or 0X prefix and a sequence of hexadecimal digits, or a 0 prefix and a sequence of octal digits." For "0x", the longest initial subsequence that is of the expected form: Is "0" a sequence of digits in base 16? Yes, '0' is a hex digit. So the subject sequence is "0". The "0x" prefix is not used because there is no sequence of hex digits after it. So endptr points to 'x'. So value 0. Good.

Now, what about "0x" with base 16 and there is a digit after prefix? "0x1": The longest initial subsequence of expected form is "0x1" (prefix + digit). So value 1. Good.

Now, what about "0x" with base 16 and there is a digit after prefix but the digit is '0'? "0x0": subject sequence "0x0", value 0. Good.

Now, what about "0x" with base 16 and there is a digit after prefix but the digit is 'a'? "0xa": value 10. Good.

Now, all good.

Now, what about `std::stoi` for base 16 with string "0x" and there is a digit after prefix but the digit is '0' and then 'x'? "0x0x": subject sequence "0x0", value 0. Our helper: prefix check true, consume prefix, collect '0', stop at 'x'. Good.

Now, all good.

Now, let's think about the `_stoi` function's overflow check. We check `value < -2147483648 or value > 2147483647`. But what about the case where the digit string is extremely long, causing Python to compute a huge integer? That's fine, but it could be slow for extremely long strings. But that's acceptable. However, to avoid huge computation, we could check overflow during accumulation, but not necessary. But if the string is millions of characters, it could be slow. But C++ would also parse until overflow? Actually strtol stops at first invalid char, but if all digits are valid, it will accumulate and may overflow long before the end, and then set errno to ERANGE and return LONG_MAX or LONG_MIN. Then stoi checks if it fits in int. So C++ would not parse the entire huge string; it would stop at the point of overflow? Actually strtol continues parsing digits even after overflow? It sets errno to ERANGE and returns the saturated value, but it still consumes all valid digits? The C standard says: "If the correct value is outside the range of representable values, LONG_MAX or LONG_MIN is returned (according to the sign of the value), and the value of the macro ERANGE is stored in errno." It doesn't say it stops early; it likely continues to consume the digits to determine the endptr. So for a huge string, it parses all digits. Our Python code also parses all digits, which could be slow but behaviorally similar. Fine.

But wait: The C++ code includes `#include <bitset>`, `<sstream>`, `<iomanip>`, `<string>`. Not relevant.

Now, let's ensure the code is syntactically correct.

```python
def _is_digit(ch, base):
    if base == 2:
        return ch == '0' or ch == '1'
    if base == 8:
        return '0' <= ch <= '7'
    if base == 16:
        return ch in '0123456789abcdefABCDEF'
    return False


def _digit_value(ch):
    if '0' <= ch <= '9':
        return ord(ch) - ord('0')
    if 'a' <= ch <= 'f':
        return ord(ch) - ord('a') + 10
    if 'A' <= ch <= 'F':
        return ord(ch) - ord('A') + 10
    raise ValueError("invalid digit")


def _stoi(s, base):
    i = 0
    n = len(s)
    while i < n and s[i] in ' \t\n\v\f\r':
        i += 1
    if i >= n:
        raise ValueError("invalid_argument")
    sign = 1
    if s[i] == '+':
        i += 1
    elif s[i] == '-':
        sign = -1
        i += 1
    if base == 16 and i + 1 < n and s[i] == '0' and s[i + 1] in 'xX':
        if i + 2 < n and _is_digit(s[i + 2], 16):
            i += 2
    digits_start = i
    while i < n and _is_digit(s[i], base):
        i += 1
    if i == digits_start:
        raise ValueError("invalid_argument")
    digit_str = s[digits_start:i]
    value = 0
    for ch in digit_str:
        value = value * base + _digit_value(ch)
    value *= sign
    if value < -2147483648 or value > 2147483647:
        raise OverflowError("out_of_range")
    return value


class NumberConverter:
    @staticmethod
    def decimal_to_binary(decimal_num):
        binary_num = format(decimal_num & 0xFFFFFFFF, '032b').lstrip('0')
        return binary_num if binary_num else "0"

    @staticmethod
    def binary_to_decimal(binary_num):
        return _stoi(binary_num, 2)

    @staticmethod
    def decimal_to_octal(decimal_num):
        return format(decimal_num, 'o')

    @staticmethod
    def octal_to_decimal(octal_num):
        return _stoi(octal_num, 8)

    @staticmethod
    def decimal_to_hex(decimal_num):
        return format(decimal_num, 'x')

    @staticmethod
    def hex_to_decimal(hex_num):
        return _stoi(hex_num, 16)
```

Now, let's double-check the `_stoi` function for base 16 with string "0x" and no digit after prefix: The prefix check condition `if i + 2 < n and _is_digit(s[i + 2], 16):` is false because i+2 == n. So we don't consume prefix. Then digits_start = i (at '0'), collect '0'. Good.

But what about string "0x" with base 16 and there is a digit after prefix but the digit is at position i+2 and i+2 < n, but the digit is a valid hex digit. We consume prefix. Then digits_start = i (now at position after prefix). But what if the digit after prefix is the only digit, and then the string ends? We collect it. Good.

Now, what about string "0x" with base 16 and there is a digit after prefix but the digit is at position i+2 and i+2 < n, but the digit is a valid hex digit, and there are more digits. We consume prefix, collect all digits. Good.

Now, what about string "0x" with base 16 and there is a digit after prefix but the digit is at position i+2 and i+2 < n, but the digit is a valid hex digit, and there is a sign after? We collect digits until sign, stop. Good.

Now, all good.

Now, let's think about the `_stoi` function for base 16 with string "0x" and there is a digit after prefix but the digit is at position i+2 and i+2 < n, but the digit is a valid hex digit, and there is a whitespace after? We collect digits until whitespace. Good.

Now, one more nuance: C++ `std::stoi` for base 16 with string "0x" and there is a digit after prefix but the digit is at position i+2 and i+2 < n, but the digit is a valid hex digit, and there is a null? We collect. Good.

Now, all good.

Now, let's consider the case where the string is "0x" and there is a digit after prefix but the digit is at position i+2 and i+2 < n, but the digit is a valid hex digit, and the string is "0x0" (i+2 < n, s[i+2]='0'). We consume prefix, digits_start at '0', collect '0'. Good.

Now, what about string "0x" and there is a digit after prefix but the digit is at position i+2 and i+2 < n, but the digit is a valid hex digit, and the string is "0x1" (i+2 < n, s[i+2]='1'). Good.

Now, all good.

Now, let's think about the `_stoi` function for base 16 with string "0x" and there is a digit after prefix but the digit is at position i+2 and i+2 < n, but the digit is a valid hex digit, and the string is "0x" followed by a newline? The newline is not a hex digit, so prefix not consumed, collect '0'. Good.

Now, all good.

Now, let's consider the `decimal_to_binary` function for negative numbers. `format(decimal_num & 0xFFFFFFFF, '032b')` for decimal_num = -1 gives "11111111111111111111111111111111". lstrip('0') returns same. Good.

But what about decimal_num = -2147483648? `-2147483648 & 0xFFFFFFFF` in Python: Since Python ints are arbitrary precision, -2147483648 & 0xFFFFFFFF = 2147483648? Let's compute: 0xFFFFFFFF = 4294967295. -2147483648 & 4294967295 = 2147483648? Because -2147483648 in two's complement 32-bit is 0x80000000 = 2147483648. Yes. format(2147483648, '032b') = "10000000000000000000000000000000". Good.

Now, what about decimal_num = 2147483648 (outside int range)? `2147483648 & 0xFFFFFFFF` = 2147483648, format gives "10000000000000000000000000000000". C++ would not accept 2147483648 as int. But if Python passes it, our function returns the low 32 bits. That's fine.

Now, all good.

Now, let's consider the `decimal_to_octal` and `decimal_to_hex` for negative numbers. Python `format(-8, 'o')` returns '-10'. C++ `std::oct << -8` returns "-10". Good. But what about `format(-8, 'o')` in Python? Yes, it includes sign. Good.

Now, what about `format(0, 'o')` -> '0'. Good.

Now, what about `format(0, 'x')` -> '0'. Good.

Now, all good.

Now, let's think about the `_stoi` function's overflow check for negative numbers. We compute `value` as positive, then multiply by sign, then check range. For value = 2147483648, sign = -1 -> -2147483648, which is within range. Good. For value = 2147483648, sign = 1 -> 2147483648, out of range. Good.

Now, what about value = 2147483648, sign = -1, but the digit string is "10000000000000000000000000000000" base 2. That's exactly INT_MIN. Good.

Now, what about value = 2147483648, sign = -1, but the digit string is "0x80000000" base 16. Same. Good.

Now, what about value = 2147483648, sign = -1, but the digit string is "20000000000" base 8. Same. Good.

Now, all good.

Now, let's consider the case where the string has a sign and the value is exactly INT_MIN. Our helper returns -2147483648. Good.

Now, what about the case where the string has a sign and the value is exactly INT_MAX. Our helper returns 2147483647. Good.

Now, what about the case where the string has a sign and the value is INT_MAX+1. Our helper raises OverflowError. Good.

Now, all good.

Now, let's think about the `_stoi` function's handling of the prefix for base 16 when the string is "0x" and there is a digit after prefix but the digit is at position i+2 and i+2 < n, but the digit is a valid hex digit, and the string is "0x" followed by a sign? Not possible because sign is not hex digit. So prefix not consumed. Good.

Now, what about the string "0x" followed by a valid hex digit but the valid hex digit is actually part of a prefix? E.g., "0x0x10". We consume prefix "0x", then digits_start at '0', collect '0', stop at 'x'. Good.

Now, all good.

Now, let's consider the `_stoi` function's handling of the prefix for base 16 when the string is "0x" and there is a digit after prefix but the digit is at position i+2 and i+2 < n, but the digit is a valid hex digit, and the string is "0x" followed by a valid hex digit and then a sign? E.g., "0x1+2". We consume prefix, collect '1', stop at '+'. Good.

Now, all good.

Now, let's think about the `_stoi` function's handling of whitespace after the sign. For example, "+ 1" base 2: after sign, i at space, digits_start at space, while loop sees space not digit, i == digits_start, raise ValueError. C++ invalid_argument. Good.

Now, what about " + 1" base 2: skip leading space, sign '+', i at space, no digits, invalid_argument. Good.

Now, all good.

=== RAW ANSWER ===
```python
def _is_digit(ch, base):
    if base == 2:
        return ch == '0' or ch == '1'
    if base == 8:
        return '0' <= ch <= '7'
    if base == 16:
        return ch in '0123456789abcdefABCDEF'
    return False


def _digit_value(ch):
    if '0' <= ch <= '9':
        return ord(ch) - ord('0')
    if 'a' <= ch <= 'f':
        return ord(ch) - ord('a') + 10
    if 'A' <= ch <= 'F':
        return ord(ch) - ord('A') + 10
    raise ValueError("invalid digit")


def _stoi(s, base):
    i = 0
    n = len(s)

    while i < n and s[i] in ' \t\n\v\f\r':
        i += 1

    if i >= n:
        raise ValueError("invalid_argument")

    sign = 1
    if s[i] == '+':
        i += 1
    elif s[i] == '-':
        sign = -1
        i += 1

    if base == 16 and i + 1 < n and s[i] == '0' and s[i + 1] in 'xX':
        if i + 2 < n and _is_digit(s[i + 2], 16):
            i += 2

    digits_start = i
    while i < n and _is_digit(s[i], base):
        i += 1

    if i == digits_start:
        raise ValueError("invalid_argument")

    digit_str = s[digits_start:i]
    value = 0
    for ch in digit_str:
        value = value * base + _digit_value(ch)

    value *= sign

    if value < -2147483648 or value > 2147483647:
        raise OverflowError("out_of_range")

    return value


class NumberConverter:
    @staticmethod
    def decimal_to_binary(decimal_num):
        binary_num = format(decimal_num & 0xFFFFFFFF, '032b').lstrip('0')
        return binary_num if binary_num else "0"

    @staticmethod
    def binary_to_decimal(binary_num):
        return _stoi(binary_num, 2)

    @staticmethod
    def decimal_to_octal(decimal_num):
        return format(decimal_num, 'o')

    @staticmethod
    def octal_to_decimal(octal_num):
        return _stoi(octal_num, 8)

    @staticmethod
    def decimal_to_hex(decimal_num):
        return format(decimal_num, 'x')

    @staticmethod
    def hex_to_decimal(hex_num):
        return _stoi(hex_num, 16)
```

=== TRANSLATED CODE (py) ===
def _is_digit(ch, base):
    if base == 2:
        return ch == '0' or ch == '1'
    if base == 8:
        return '0' <= ch <= '7'
    if base == 16:
        return ch in '0123456789abcdefABCDEF'
    return False


def _digit_value(ch):
    if '0' <= ch <= '9':
        return ord(ch) - ord('0')
    if 'a' <= ch <= 'f':
        return ord(ch) - ord('a') + 10
    if 'A' <= ch <= 'F':
        return ord(ch) - ord('A') + 10
    raise ValueError("invalid digit")


def _stoi(s, base):
    i = 0
    n = len(s)

    while i < n and s[i] in ' \t\n\v\f\r':
        i += 1

    if i >= n:
        raise ValueError("invalid_argument")

    sign = 1
    if s[i] == '+':
        i += 1
    elif s[i] == '-':
        sign = -1
        i += 1

    if base == 16 and i + 1 < n and s[i] == '0' and s[i + 1] in 'xX':
        if i + 2 < n and _is_digit(s[i + 2], 16):
            i += 2

    digits_start = i
    while i < n and _is_digit(s[i], base):
        i += 1

    if i == digits_start:
        raise ValueError("invalid_argument")

    digit_str = s[digits_start:i]
    value = 0
    for ch in digit_str:
        value = value * base + _digit_value(ch)

    value *= sign

    if value < -2147483648 or value > 2147483647:
        raise OverflowError("out_of_range")

    return value


class NumberConverter:
    @staticmethod
    def decimal_to_binary(decimal_num):
        binary_num = format(decimal_num & 0xFFFFFFFF, '032b').lstrip('0')
        return binary_num if binary_num else "0"

    @staticmethod
    def binary_to_decimal(binary_num):
        return _stoi(binary_num, 2)

    @staticmethod
    def decimal_to_octal(decimal_num):
        return format(decimal_num, 'o')

    @staticmethod
    def octal_to_decimal(octal_num):
        return _stoi(octal_num, 8)

    @staticmethod
    def decimal_to_hex(decimal_num):
        return format(decimal_num, 'x')

    @staticmethod
    def hex_to_decimal(hex_num):
        return _stoi(hex_num, 16)
